now only depends on the isomorphism type
[H]
of
H
for all
H\sqsubseteq G_{\Phi}
.
By induction we see that the number

\big|\big\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}\,:\,\textup{{perc}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)=H\big\}\big|

also depends only on the isomorphism type of
H
, whenever
H\sqsubseteq G_{\Phi}
.
Denote this number by
\alpha_{[H]}
and set
\Psi([H]):=\Phi(H)
.
This is only well-defined for
H\sqsubseteq G_{\Phi}
, thus we consider the domain of
\Psi
to be
\mathcal{P}([G_{\Phi}])=\mathcal{P}([G])
.
This means that (
3
) simplifies:

\Psi([H])\ =\ \sum_{[F]}\#\mathrm{Ind}([F]\to[H])\alpha_{[F]}

(4)

where the sum is over all isomorphism classes
[F]
of induced subgraphs
F\sqsubseteq H
.
This implies that
\Phi
is
\mathcal{P}(G)
-good.

It remains to define the function
\mathcal{P}(G)\to{\mathcal{OG}_{=j}}
.
Let
H\sqsubseteq G
, then
H):={\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(H_{\Phi})
for some
H_{\Phi}\sqsubseteq G_{\Phi}
with
H\cong H_{\Phi}
.
The property
\varphi(H))=\varphi(H)
now directly follows from
\mathcal{S}\!I
being a set-instantiator.
∎

Note that Lemma
22
only guarantees the local behaviour of
M
to be
\mathcal{P}(G)
-good.
This leaves two possibilities for contradictions: Either the local behaviour is even
(\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}})
-good, or the function grows too quickly for inputs with many isolated vertices.
This next Witness Theorem shows that in the first case we can always find an ordered graph that proves that
M
does not compute the correct function using a simple linear algebra argument.

Theorem 23

(The Witness Theorem)
.

Fix a bad
\varphi:{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}\to\mathbb{N}
.
Then there exists
G\in{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
such that for
every
(\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}})
-good function
\Psi:(\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}})\to\mathbb{N}
,
there exists
W\in\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}

with
\Psi(W)\neq\varphi(W)
.

Proof.

Choose
G
to be the disjoint union of the graphs in
\textup{supp}(\varphi)
and let
\mathcal{OG}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
, keeping only one representative per isomorphism class.
By Fact
9
, the functions
\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to\,\star\,)
for
H\in\mathcal{OG}^{\prime}
are linearly independent.
Thus the patterns and coefficients of any function
\Psi
with support
\textup{supp}(\Psi)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
are uniquely determined by the evaluations
\Psi
on
\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
, and there exists a witness
W\in\mathcal{P}(G)\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
with
\Psi(W)\neq\varphi(W)
, since
\Psi
is good while
\varphi
is bad (thus, in particular,
\Psi
and
\varphi
are not the same function).
∎

With this last tool we are finally able to lead the existence of polynomial-time NTMs computing bad ordered graph motif parameters to a contradiction.

Lemma 24
.

Let
M
be any nondeterministic polynomial-time Turing machine computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
for some bad pure ordered graph motif parameter
\varphi
.
Then there is a
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in{\mathcal{OG}_{=j}}
such that
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{W}}(j)\neq\varphi(W)
.

Proof.

We invoke the Witness Theorem
23
with our
\varphi

to obtain an
G_{1}\in{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
as in the theorem.
Construct
G_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to G_{2})

\displaystyle>\max\big\{\varphi(F)\,:\,F\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}\big\}

for every
H\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\setminus{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}

(5)

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to G_{2})

\displaystyle=\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to G_{1})

for every
H\in{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}

(6)

by adding sufficiently many isolated vertices to
G_{1}
.
For this it is sufficient to add
1+\max\big\{\varphi(F)\,:\,F\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}\big\}
isolated vertices in between every pair of adjacent vertices in
G_{1}
, relative to
\leq_{G_{1}}
.

We now use Lemma
22
to obtain
j\in\mathbb{N}
, a function
\mathcal{P}(G_{2})\to{\mathcal{OG}_{=j}}
and a
\mathcal{P}(G_{2})
-good function
\Psi
with
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{H)}}(j)=\Psi(H)
and
\varphi(H))=\varphi(H)
for every
H\sqsubseteq G_{2}
.
There are now two possibilities to find the required counterexample
W
:
If there are no basis functions present in
\Psi
with a pattern in
\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\setminus{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
, we can invoke the Witness Theorem again, however if any of these basis functions are present we can construct a direct counterexample.

We first handle the case where such a basis function is present, so let the coefficient of
\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to\,\star\,)
in
\Psi
be positive for some
H\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\setminus{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
.
Then

\Psi(G_{2})\geq\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\to G_{2})>\max\big\{\varphi(F)\,:\,F\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}\big\}\geq\varphi(G_{1})=\varphi(G_{2})

by (
5
) and (
6
), combined with the fact that
\varphi
is pure,
and our counterexample is
W:=G_{2}
.

Otherwise assume that no such basis function is present.
Then
\widetilde{\Psi}
obtained from
\Psi
by restricting to
\mathcal{P}(G_{1})
(i.e. setting all coefficients of
\mathcal{P}(G_{2})\setminus\mathcal{P}(G_{1})
to zero), is
(\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}})
-good, which is a requirement for the second part of the Witness Theorem
23
(we already used it to obtain
G_{1}
).
Furthermore
\widetilde{\Psi}_{|\mathcal{P}(G_{1})}=\Psi_{|\mathcal{P}(G_{1})}
.
We invoke the second part of the Witness Theorem
23
to find a point

W\in\mathcal{P}(G_{1})\cap{\mathcal{OG}^{\text{pure}}}
with
\Psi(W)=\widetilde{\Psi}(W)\neq\varphi(W)
which is our counterexample.

Independent of how we have obtained our
W
we observe

\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{W)}}(j)=\Psi(W)\neq\varphi(W)=\varphi(W))
,
which completes the proof for
W^{\prime}=W)
.
∎

This proves the separation
in Theorem
16
, which together with Lemmas
11
and
12
finishes the proof of the theorem.

6
Overview of Results for Relational Structures and Categories

Induced subgraph counts can also be considered in a colored setting, where each vertex has a color and colored graphs are considered isomorphic if the colors of the vertices that are mapped to each other by the isomorphism have to agree.
Such colored patterns occur, e.g., within the proofs of some parameterized hardness results
[
10
,
7
]
, as access to vertex-colors gives more versatility in the reduction.
It is natural to ask whether our main theorem generalizes from graphs to colored graphs and to other more general versions of graphs, such as hypergraphs of bounded edge-cardinality. We answer this question positively for general
relational structures
; due to space limitations, this part appears as Appendix
B
. The proof proceeds along the same lines as Theorem
13
, our main theorem about graphs.

Generalizing further from relational structures, we consider the vastly more general setting of
category theory
in Appendix
C
and show a variant of Theorem
13
for motif parameters defined over categories.
It turns out that the arguments from the main proof can be adapted for this purpose, but only after significant technical effort: While homomorphisms and embeddings naturally generalize from graphs to categories, nontrivial concepts from category theory are needed to formulate the proper requirements on categories that allow us to handle subobjects similarly to induced subgraphs.
Among other such requirements, we require
well-powered
categories (where every subobject itself has only finitely many isomorphism types of subobjects) and
proper factorization systems
(that decompose morphisms into compositions of epimorphisms and monomorphisms).
We then use the general category-theoretic framework to study combinatorial interpretations of counting problems related to finite vector spaces and to so-called
parameter sets
, which play a role in enumerative combinatorics.

Appendix A
Omitted proofs

Proof of Fact
9
.

We show the fact for unordered graphs; the version for ordered graphs is shown along the same lines.
Let
\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{t}\}
for
t\in\mathbb{N}
be an enumeration of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
|V(G_{i})|<|V(G_{j})|
implies
i<j
.
Consider the matrix
A
whose rows and columns are indexed by
G_{1},\ldots,G_{t}
, with entries
\#\mathrm{Ind}(G_{i}\mathop{\to}G_{j})
.

We show that this matrix is lower triangular with all diagonal entries equal to
1
, implying full rank and thus linear independence of the functions
f_{H}
:
Consider indices
i,j
with
i\geq j
:
First, we have
|V(G_{i})|\geq|V(G_{j})|
by the choice of enumeration. If
A(i,j)\neq 0
holds, it must follow that
|V(G_{i})|=|V(G_{j})|
, as otherwise
G_{i}
has too many vertices to be an induced subgraph of
G_{j}
. But then
A(i,j)\neq 0
only for
j=i
. In this case,
A(i,j)=1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12
.

Since
\varphi
is a positive integer linear combination of finitely many induced subgraph numbers, and
{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
is closed under such linear combinations, it suffices to consider
\varphi(G)=\#\mathrm{Ind}(H\mathop{\to}G)
for
H\in\mathcal{G}
.
We show the statement for unordered graphs, the proof for ordered graphs is analogous.

A nondeterministic Turing machine for
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
proceeds as follows, for hardcoded
H
with
|V(H)|=k
:
Given
(x,O)
encoding a graph
G
, nondeterministically guess strings
x_{1}<\ldots<x_{k}<x
of length
|x|
, where
<
denotes the lexicographic ordering on strings.
Then, for every
\{i,j\}\in\binom{k}{2}
, write
x_{i}x_{j}
on the oracle tape and query
O
to obtain a bit
a_{i,j}
.
Finally, test by brute-force whether the graph with adjacency matrix given by
a_{i,j}
is isomorphic to
H
; this only requires constant time, as
H
is fixed.
If the test succeeds, then this branch accepts, otherwise it rejects.
This nondeterministic Turing machine clearly runs in polynomial-time on every branch, and the number of accepting computation paths equals the number of vertex-subsets
S
of
G
such that
G[S]
is isomorphic to
H
.
∎

Appendix B
Relational Structures

First, we need to transfer some terminology from graphs to relational structures and introduce some additional notions that are specific for relational structures. Let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell}>0
.
A finite relational structure of
type

\Delta:=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell})
consists of a finite universe
V
of individuals (which we call vertices, in analogy to our terminology for graphs) and relations
R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell}
with
R_{i}\subseteq\binom{V}{k_{i}}
.
Denote the class of all finite relational structures of type
\Delta
as
\mathrm{Rel}(\Delta)
.
From now on, we will refer to finite relational structures simply as relational structures.
For any relational structure
A=(V,R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell})
, we write
V(A):=V
and
R_{i}(A)=R_{i}
for
i=1\ldots\ell
.

A relational structure
A
is an
induced substructure
of
B
, written as
A\sqsubseteq B
, if
V(A)\subseteq V(B)
and
R_{i}(A)=R_{i}(B)\cap\binom{V(A)}{k_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
.
Furthermore
A
is a
substructure
of
B
, written as
A\subseteq
B, if
V(A)\subseteq V(B)
and
R_{i}(A)\subseteq R_{i}(B)\cap\binom{V(A)}{k_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
.
A function
f:V(A)\to V(B)
is called a
strong embedding
if
f
is injective and
X\in R_{i}(A)\Leftrightarrow f(Y)\in R_{i}(B)
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
X\subseteq V(A)
.

A relational structure
A
is called
irreducible
if for all different
u,v\in V(A)
there is some
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
X\in R_{i}(A)
with
u,v\in X
.

A vertex
v\in V(A)
is called
P
-padding for
P=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
if for all
p_{i}=0
we have
\{X\in R_{i}(A):v\in X\}=\emptyset
and for all
p_{i}=1
we have
\{X\in R_{i}(A):v\in X\}=\{X\in\binom{V(A)}{k_{i}}:v\in X\}
, i.e.,
v
is not in a relation with any other vertices for
p_{i}=0
and in all possible relations with other vertices for
p_{i}=1
.
A relational structure
A
is now called
P
-pure if it does not contain any
P
-padding vertices.

A subset
F\subseteq\mathrm{Rel}(\Delta)
is called
upwards closed
if for every
A\in F
and all
B\in\mathrm{Rel}(\Delta)
with
V(A)=V(B)
and
A\subseteq B
we have
B\in F
.
Let
F\subseteq\mathrm{Rel}(\Delta)
be a potentially empty upwards closed set of
P
-pure irreducible relational structures of type
\Delta
.
Now let
U=\mathrm{Forb}(F)
be the set of all relational structures of type
\Delta
that do not contain any substructure isomorphic to any element in
F
.
This is equivalent to
U
not containing any induced substructures isomorphic to elements in
F
, due to
F
being upwards closed.
Restricting
U
to only the
P
-pure relational structures yields
{U}^{\text{$P$-pure}}
.

As was the case for graphs, the Ramsey theorem only holds for an ordered version of relational structures.
We say
\mathrm{ORel}(\Delta)
is the class of all ordered relational structures of type
\Delta
.
Ordered relational structures are defined, as in the graph case, by incorporating a linear order
\leq_{V}
on the universe into the structure and extending notions of homomorphisms, (strong) embeddings, and isomorphisms to respect the ordering, which also yields notions of (induced) substructures for ordered structures.
For a relational structure
A
and
X\subseteq V(A)
, write
A[X]
for the substructure induced by
X
.
Parallel to Definition
10
, define (ordered) motif parameters
\varphi
to be linear combinations of induced substructure counts
\#\mathrm{Ind}(A\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
for fixed (ordered) relational structures
A
.
We call
\varphi

P
-pure if all its patterns are
P
-pure. For a class of relational structures
U
, we speak of an
U
motif parameter if its domain is restricted to
U
.

Relational structures
A
with
V(A)=\{0,\ldots,j-1\}
are encoded as oracles
(x,O)
by choosing
x=j
, and by querying
O
with
(i,(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}}))
, a Turing machine can ask whether
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}}\}\in R_{i}(A)
.

We can now state our main theorem, in analogy to Theorem
16
.

Theorem 25
.

Let
\varphi:U\to\mathbb{N}
be a
P
-pure ordered
U
motif parameter.
Then
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
iff all coefficients of
\varphi
in the
\#\mathrm{Ind}(A_{i}\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
basis are nonnegative integers.

We remind that
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
is a promise problem.
As such the NTM only has to count correctly whenever the pair
(x,O)
correctly encodes an ordered relational structure from the domain of
\varphi
, in this case
U
.
This for example implies that for
\Delta=(2)
,
P=(0)
and
F=\{K_{3}\}
(the clique on 3 vertices), the counter example oracle input for each NTM will itself be a graph without any triangles.

The proof of Theorem
25
is almost identical to that of Theorem
16
, so we will only point out some specific modifications required in the proof:

•

The Ramsey theorem: Lemma
20
was shown in
[
29
]
not only for the class of all finite graphs, but more generally for the class of all relational structures defined via a set of irreducible forbidden substructures, such as
U
.

•

The value of a
P
-pure ordered motif parameter
\varphi
does not change by adding or removing
P
-padding vertices, since any
P
-padding vertex
v
in an ordered relational structure stays a
P
-padding vertex in any induced substructure containing
v
.
Anywhere we were filling up a graph with isolated vertices, we instead add
P
-padding vertices. (Note that isolated vertices are simply
(0)
-padding vertices.)

•

By the same argument, it is impossible to leave
U
by adding
P
-padding vertices to a relational structure that was in
U
.

•

Due to the changes of the way we store an ordered relational structure as an oracle, we will be showing the existence of set-instantiators explicitly again.

The notion of a set-instantiator is generalized as follows. We write
U_{=j}
for the ordered relations in
U
whose universes are exactly the vertices
\{0,\ldots,j-1\}
with the natural order on them.

Definition 26

(Set-instantiator against
(M,A,\varphi)
)
.

Let
M
be a nondeterministic Turing machine,
let
A\in U_{=j}
and
let
\varphi
be an ordered
P
-pure motif parameter.

Let
\top
be a symbolic top element above the induced substructure poset
\mathcal{P}(A)
, i.e.,
\top\not\sqsubseteq B
for all induced substructures
B\sqsubseteq A
.
A
set-instantiator

\mathcal{S}\!I
is a triple of

\circ
some
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\circ
an instantiation function
\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}:\mathcal{P}(A)\to U_{=j}
, and

\circ
a perception function
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\mathcal{P}(A)\cup\{\top\}
,

such that the following property holds for all induced substructures
B\sqsubseteq A
:

\bullet

\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}
is an accepting path for the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)}}(j)

if and only if
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\sqsubseteq B
, and

\bullet

\varphi(\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)))=\varphi(B)
.

Lemma 27
.

Let
\varphi:U\to\mathbb{N}
be a
P
-pure ordered
U
motif parameter, let
M
be a polynomial-time NTM computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
and let
A\in U
.
Then there is a set-instantiator against
(M,A,\varphi)
.

Proof.

For now let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be undetermined, we will choose it accordingly later.
Uniformly at random choose a function
\hat{\xi}:V(A)\to\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
and let it induce a monotone function
\xi:V(A)\to\{0,\ldots,|V(A)|\cdot n-1\}
by mapping the
i
-th smallest vertex
v\in V(A)
to
(i-1)\cdot n+\hat{\xi}(v)
.
We then set
j=|V(A)|\cdot n
.

For an ordered relational structure
B\sqsubseteq A
, we denote by
\xi(B)
the relational structure on the vertices
\{0,\ldots,|V(G)|\cdot n-1\}
, where the relations are given as the images of the relations of
B
under
\xi
.
Furthermore, for every vertex
v
that is not in the image of
\xi(V(A))
, make
v
a
P
-padding vertex.
We are trying to find some
\xi
, s.t. for all
B\sqsubseteq A
, all accepting paths of the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\xi(B)}}(j)
do not access the oracle for any relations incident to vertices in
\xi(V(A))\setminus\xi(V(B))
.
By the union bound, if we can show this happening with high probability for each of the finitely many individual
B\sqsubseteq A
, then there is such a
\xi
, so for now fix some
B\sqsubseteq A
.

Now group the functions
\xi
by their image of
V(B)
and fix one such group
I
.
Note that the image of the remaining vertices
V(A)\setminus V(B)
under
\xi
is still independently and uniformly distributed from
n
possible choices each by the choice of
\hat{\xi}
.
Consider the set
S_{I}
of all vertices queried by all accepting paths of the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\xi(B)}}(j)
.
Let
t_{M}(j)
is the worst-case running-time of
M
on input
j
.
Each oracle query queries one of the relations at a time and thus at most
\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell})
vertices, there are at most
\varphi(B)
many accepting paths and each accepting path can query the oracle at most
t_{M}(j)
many times and as such the size
|S_{I}|
is bounded by
\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell})\cdot\varphi(B)\cdot t_{M}(j)
, which is polynomial in
\log(j)
due to
M
being polynomial-time and
B
and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell}
being fixed.
As a rough estimate using the Bernoulli inequality and
|V(A)\setminus V(B)|\leq|V(A)|
, this means that for a fraction of at least
\left(1-\frac{|S_{I}|}{n}\right)^{|V(A)|}\geq 1-\frac{|S_{I}|\cdot|V(A)|}{n}
choices of
\hat{\xi}
resulting in
\xi\in I
, none of the accepting paths queries any edges incident to vertices in
\xi(V(A))\setminus\xi(V(B))
.
By averaging across all possible
\hat{\xi}
(no longer restricted to
I
), we see that a fraction of
1-\frac{\mathrm{polylog}(j)\cdot|V(A)|}{n}
choices have our desired property.

Using the union bound over all
B\sqsubseteq A
, we get that a fraction of
1-|\mathcal{P}(A)|\cdot\frac{\mathrm{polylog}(j)\cdot|V(A)|}{n}
choices of
\hat{\xi}
lead to the desired property for all
B\sqsubseteq A
simultaneously.
By choosing
n
large enough (remember that
j
and
n
are polynomially related, so this is always possible), this guarantees the existence of our desired
\xi
, s.t. for all
B\sqsubseteq A
, all accepting paths of the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\xi(B)}}(j)
do not access the oracle for any edges incident to vertices in
\xi(V(A))\setminus\xi(V(B))
.

We can now construct our set-instantiator with
j
chosen as above.
The instantiation function for
B\sqsubseteq A
is

\displaystyle\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)

\displaystyle:=\xi(B)\,.

For any computation path
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\star}
of a computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\xi(B)}}(j)
, denote by
S_{\tau}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,j-1\}
the set of vertices that are queried by the computation.
We set

\displaystyle\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau):=\begin{cases}A[\xi^{-1}(S_{\tau})]&\text{if $\tau$ is an accepting path of the computation $\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\xi(A)}}(j)$,}\\
\top&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

It remains to check the properties of a set-instantiator.
Clearly
\varphi(\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B))=\varphi(B)
for all
B\sqsubseteq G
, since the two relational structures are identical except
P
-padding vertices and no pattern in
\varphi
contains any
P
-padding vertices.
Further we need that for all
B\sqsubseteq A
we have that
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\star}
is an accepting path for the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)}}(j)
iff
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\sqsubseteq B
.
For this fix
B\sqsubseteq A
and let
\tau
be an accepting path for the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)}}(j)
.
By our choice of
\xi
we know that no other vertices of
\xi(V(A))\setminus\xi(V(B))
are queried, or in other words,
\xi^{-1}(S_{\tau})\subseteq V(B)
and thus
\tau
is also an accepting path for the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(A)}}(j)
.
This directly implies
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)=A[\xi^{-1}(S_{\tau})]=\sqsubseteq B
.
On the other hand, let
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\sqsubseteq B
, then
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\neq\top
and
\tau
is an accepting path of the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(A)}}(j)
, since
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)=A[\xi^{-1}(S_{\tau})]\sqsubseteq B
,
\tau
is also an accepting path of the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(B)}}(j)
.
∎

B.1
Directed Relational Structures

So far for all of our graphs and relational structures we have assumed that they are undirected and have no self-loops or relations with duplicate vertices.

We study two further generalizations to ordered and unordered relational structures
A
, where each relation
R_{i}
can now be one of following different variants:

1.

Set relations, i.e.
R_{i}\subseteq\binom{V}{k_{i}}
which is the kind of relational structures we have studied so far.

2.

Multiset relations, i.e.
R_{i}
is a set of multiset of vertices from
V
with
k_{i}
total (not necessarily distinct) elements each.
These for example would allow us to express undirected self-loops in the graph setting.

3.

List relations without repetitions, i.e.
R_{i}
is a tuple of
k_{i}
vertices from
V
, but without repetitions.
Directed graphs without self-loops fall under this category.

4.

List relations with repetitions, i.e.
R_{i}
is a tuple of
k_{i}
elements from
V
with repetitions.
Directed graphs with self-loops fall under this category.

We can even allow different relations to be of different variants.
We call such an (ordered) relational structure a mixed (ordered) relational structure.
A
mixed type

\widetilde{\Delta}
is now a type
\Delta
, combined with the information about which variant each relation uses.
The class
\mathrm{MORel}(\widetilde{\Delta})
is then the class of all mixed ordered relational structures of type
\widetilde{\Delta}
.
The induced substructures of a mixed (ordered) relational structure are now defined in the natural way and homomorphisms and (strong) embeddings preserve the order and multiplicities of the mixed relations.
We call a mixed (ordered) relational structure
A
irreducible if for every different
u,v\in V(A)
there is some
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
X\in R_{i}(A)
, such that
X
contains both
u
and
v
(as a set, multiset or list, depending on the variant of
R_{i}
).
For a vertex
v
to be a
P
-padding vertex the relation
R_{i}
needs to contain all possible entries including
v
if
p_{i}=1
and for
p_{i}=0
it has to contain none of the entries including
v
.
As usual
A
is called
P
-pure if it contains no
P
-padding vertices.

In an ordered relational structure, each of these types of relations can be expressed by using one or more set relations in the following way.

Multiset relation:

Replace
R_{i}
by one set relation
(R_{i})_{C}
of arity
|C|
for every composition
C
of
k_{i}
.
Let
e
be a hyperedge in
R_{i}
with vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{\nu}
with multiplicities
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\nu}
.
W.l.o.g. assume that
v_{1}\leq v_{2}\leq\ldots\leq v_{\nu}
according to the linear order on
V
.
Then
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\nu}\}
is added to
(R_{i})_{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\nu})}
.

List relation without repetition:

Replace
R_{i}
by one set relation
(R_{i})_{\prec}
of arity
k_{i}
for every possible total order
\prec
of
\{1,\ldots,k_{i}\}
.
Let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}})\in R_{i}
.
Define the order
\prec_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}})}
on
\{1,\ldots,k_{i}\}
via
i\prec j:=v_{i}\leq v_{j}
and add
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}}\}
to
(R_{i})_{\prec_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k_{i}})}}
.

List relation with repetition:

Same as above, however
\prec
is now a total preorder, i.e. it does not have to be anti-symmetric.
This also implies that not all of the added relations have arity exactly
k_{i}
, but rather arity
\leq k_{i}
, similarly to the multiset relation case.

Denote the resulting type of the ordered relational structures as
\Delta^{\prime}
.
Note that
\Delta^{\prime}
only depends on
\widetilde{\Delta}
.
We call the function applying the above conversion rules
\textup{{{conv}}}:\mathrm{MORel}(\widetilde{\Delta})\to\mathrm{ORel}(\Delta^{\prime})
.

Fact 28
.

This construction is bijective, i.e. the conversion mapping
conv
is bijective.
Furthermore, both
conv
and
\textup{{{conv}}}^{-1}
are efficiently computable in the sense that it is possible, in polynomial time in
\log(|V(A)|)
, to compute the image (or preimage) of any possible entry of the relations under
conv
.

Fact 29
.

Moreover
conv
commutes with (strong) embeddings and thus induced subgraphs and isomorphisms.
In particular this directly implies that
\#\mathrm{Ind}(B\to A)=\#\mathrm{Ind}(\textup{{{conv}}}(B)\to\textup{{{conv}}}(A))
.

These two facts also imply a further generalization of the Ramsey theorem in
[
29
]
to mixed ordered relational structures by applying
conv
before using the Ramsey theorem on ordered relational structures and then applying
\textup{{{conv}}}^{-1}
to get convert the resulting relational structure back to a mixed ordered relational structure.
However we do not use this variant of the Ramsey theorem here and instead do a direct reduction.

Theorem 30
.

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}
be a mixed type with
\ell
relations, let
P\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and let
F\subseteq\mathrm{MORel}(\widetilde{\Delta})
be an upwards closed subset of
P
-pure irreducible mixed ordered relational structures.

Let
\varphi:\mathrm{Forb}(F)\to\mathbb{N}
be a
P
-pure ordered
\mathrm{Forb}(F)
motif parameter, i.e.,

\varphi(B)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\alpha_{i}\cdot\#\mathrm{Ind}(A_{i}\to B)

for pairwise non-isomorphic
P
-pure
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
and coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then the evaluation problem
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
is in
\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
iff all coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
are nonnegative integers.

Proof.

Let
F^{\prime}=\{\textup{{{conv}}}(A):A\in F\}
and let
P^{\prime}
be obtained from
P
by setting
p^{\prime}_{j_{i}}=p_{i}
where
j_{i}
ranges over all the relations added by the conversion from relation
i
.
Then
F^{\prime}
is upwards closed and only contains
P^{\prime}
-pure ordered relational structures.
We now define
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathrm{Forb}(F^{\prime})\to\mathbb{N}
as
\varphi^{\prime}(B^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\alpha_{i}\cdot\#\mathrm{Ind}(\textup{{{conv}}}(A_{i})\to B^{\prime})
.
All the
\textup{{{conv}}}(A_{i})
are pairwise non-isomorphic and
P^{\prime}
-pure.
By Theorem
25
we know that
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
iff
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}
are nonnegative integers.
Given an oracle encoding some
B\in\mathrm{Forb}(F)
we can now in polynomial time simulate an oracle encoding
\textup{{{conv}}}(B)\in\mathrm{Forb}(F^{\prime})
by using Fact
28
.
Moreover by Fact
29

\varphi(B)=\varphi^{\prime}(\textup{{{conv}}}(B))
, thus if
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
, then also
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.

Since
conv
is bijective and efficiently computable in both directions this argument also reverses and proves
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\Leftrightarrow\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.
∎

In the same way that we have proven Theorem
13
from Theorem
16
, we also get a variant with unordered mixed relational structures.

Theorem 31
.

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}
be a mixed type with
\ell
relations, let
P\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and let
F\subseteq\mathrm{MRel}(\widetilde{\Delta})
be an upwards closed subset of
P
-pure irreducible mixed relational structures.

Let
\varphi:\mathrm{Forb}(F)\to\mathbb{N}
be a
P
-pure
\mathrm{Forb}(F)
motif parameter, i.e.,

\varphi(B)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\alpha_{i}\cdot\#\mathrm{Ind}(A_{i}\to B)

for pairwise non-isomorphic
P
-pure
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
and coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then the evaluation problem
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
is in
\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
iff all coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
are nonnegative integers.

Proof.

We observe

\#\mathrm{Ind}((V_{i},R_{i,1},\ldots,R_{i,\ell})\to(V,R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell}))=\sum_{\leq_{V_{i}}}\#\mathrm{Ind}((V_{i},R_{i,1},\ldots,R_{i,\ell},\leq_{V_{i}})\mathop{\to}(V,R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell},\leq_{V}))

where
\leq_{V}
is any arbitrary linear ordering of
V
and
\leq_{V_{i}}
sums over all linear orders of
V_{i}
that result in non-isomorphic ordered relational structures.
Further let
F^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{MORel}(\Delta)
be obtained from the elements of
F
combined with all possible linear orders of their vertices.
Note that all elements of
F^{\prime}
are irreducible and
P
-pure and
F^{\prime}
is still upwards closed, we can thus invoke Theorem
30
.

The same argument as for Theorem
13
finishes the proof.
∎

B.2
Applications

For colored graphs, a special case of relational structures, we get a particularly clean dichotomy, here we do not rely on the patterns not containing any isolated vertices, we only require that there is some color that can be used as a color in the target graph that does not appear in any of the pattern graphs.
Let
\mathrm{Col}\mathcal{G}(c+1)
to be the set of all colored graphs on
c+1
colors.
We rephrase
\mathrm{Col}\mathcal{G}(c+1)
as relational structures with
\Delta=(2,\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{c\text{\ times}})
, by adding a unary relation for every color, except the color
0
.
By choosing
P=(0,\ldots,0)
we set
P
-padding vertices to be exactly those vertices using the color
0
.
A colored graph is thus
P
-pure iff it contains no vertices with color
0
.
Furthermore we set
F
to be the set of relational structures on a single vertex, such that at least two different of the unary relations are set, these correspond to vertices that are assigned more than one color, turning
\mathrm{Forb}(F)
into exactly the set of colored graphs where each vertex has exactly one color, either one of
1,\ldots,c
or the color
0
by absence of any other color.

By using Theorem
31
we thus get

Theorem 32
.

Let
\varphi:\mathrm{Col}\mathcal{G}(c+1)\to\mathbb{N}
be a colored graph motif parameter, i.e.,

\varphi(G)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\alpha_{i}\cdot\#\mathrm{Ind}(H_{i}\mathop{\to}G)

for pairwise non-isomorphic graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{s}
using the colors
\{1,\ldots,c\}
and coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then the evaluation problem
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
is in
\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
iff all coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
are nonnegative integers.

Note that while we restrict the number of colors within the theorem itself, the same result holds true if the number of colors is allowed to grow with
\varphi
, by simply choosing the number of colors for the target graph to be one bigger than the fixed number of colors used in the patterns of
\varphi
.

Appendix C
Categorical Generalization

In the proofs of §
5
we didn’t use particularly many properties of ordered graphs to show our lower bound, except some structure of (induced) subobjects, a Ramsey property and the existence of set-instantiators.
The goal of this section is now to generalize the proof and make it more explicit what properties in detail are needed for our proof to work.

C.1
Basic Category Theory

We follow the definitions of
[
36
]
and refer the reader to it should they want more than just these basic definitions.
A category
C
consists of two collections:

•

A collection of
objects
.

•

A collection of
morphisms
between objects.

such that:

•

Every morphism
f
has a specified
domain
and a
codomain
.
We write
f:a\to b
if
f
has domain
a
and codomain
b
.
Additionally we write
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}f=a
and
\mathop{\mathrm{cod}}f=b
.

•

For every object
a
there is an
identity morphism

\mathrm{id}_{a}:a\to a
.

•

For every two morphisms
f:a\to b
and
g:b\to c
, there is a specific
composite morphism

gf:a\to c
.

•

For every morphism
f:a\to b
both
f\mathrm{id}_{a}
and
\mathrm{id}_{b}f
are identical to
f
, i.e. the identity morphisms are the neutral elements of composition.

•

For every triple of morphisms
f:a\to b
,
g:b\to c
and
h:c\to d
we have that
h(gf)
and
(hg)f
are identical, i.e. composition is associative.

A morphism
f:b\to c
is called

•

a
monomorphism
, if for any morphisms
g,h:a\to b
with
fg=fh
we already have
g=h
.
We also say that
f
is monic or a mono.

•

an
epimorphism
, if for any morphisms
g,h:c\to d
with
gf=hf
we already have
g=h
.
We also say that
f
is epic or an epi.

•

an
isomorphism
, if there is a morphism
g:c\to b
with
fg=\mathrm{id}_{c}
and
gf=\mathrm{id}_{b}
.
In this case we call
b
and
c

isomorphic
, also written as
b\cong c
.

If for every two objects
a,b
the collection of all morphism from
a
to
b
is a set
4
4

4

We don’t want to to get into the set versus class discussion too much here. While some of our categories have a class worth of objects, they are essentially small, meaning there is only a set worth of non-isomorphic objects. Further all of our categories are locally small.
, then
C
is called
locally small
.
If this collection is even finite, then it is called
locally finite
.

We call some category
D
a
subcategory
of
C
if the objects of
D
are a subcollection of the objects of
C
and the morphisms of
D
are a subcollection of the morphisms of
C
.
Note that this includes all relevant identity and composite morphisms in order for
D
to be a category.
We say
D
is a
full subcategory
of
C
if, additionally, for every two objects
a,b
in
D
, it contains all morphisms with domain
a
and codomain
b
from
C
.

The last concept we need is the concept of finite coproducts.
For objects
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
we say that an object
b
, together with morphisms
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{k}
where
\iota_{i}:a_{i}\to b
, called the
coproduct injections
5
5

5

These coproduct injections do not have to be injective, nor mono
, is the
coproduct
of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
, if for all morphisms
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
with
f_{i}:a_{i}\to c
there is a unique morphism
f:b\to c
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}

The object
b
is unique up to isomorphism, so we write
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
for it.
We also write
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}
for the morphism
f
.

C.2
Counting in Categories

Our objects of consideration are now objects of some category
C
.
To specify the subobjects we are counting, we designate a class of morphisms from
C
:

Definition 33
.

Let
C
be a locally small category and let
\mathcal{M}
be some class of morphisms in
C
that contains all isomorphisms and is closed under composition.
For any objects
a,b
we call morphisms
a\to b
from
\mathcal{M}
the
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects of
a
under
b
.
We identify morphisms
f,f^{\prime}:a\to b
if there is an isomorphism
g:a\to a
with
f^{\prime}=gf
.
The set of all
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects of
a
under
b
is denoted by
\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}b)
, while the class of all
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects under
b
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}(b)
.
If all such
\mathcal{P}(b)
are small (i.e. a set), then we call
C

\mathcal{M}
-well-powered and if they are all finite
C
is called finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered.
For notational convenience, we write
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b):=\{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}f\,:\,f\in\mathcal{P}(b)\}
.

Let
f:a\to b
and
f^{\prime}:a^{\prime}\to b
be
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects under
b
.
We write
f^{\prime}\prec f
if there is some morphism
g
with
f^{\prime}=gf
.

The class
\mathcal{M}
in here should be thought of as some class of special monomorphisms.
In the example of undirected graphs
\mathcal{M}
is the set of strong graph embeddings, i.e. injective graph homomorphisms that preserve both edges and non-edges.
The poset
\mathcal{P}(G)
is then the set of all induced subgraphs of
G
(represented via their graph embeddings), while
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(G)
contains all graphs with isomorphism types that are among the induced subgraphs of
G
.

Combined with a second class of morphisms
\mathcal{E}
, we obtain a factorization system:

Definition 34
.

Let
C
be a category and let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
be some classes of morphisms in
C
.
We call (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
) a factorization system if they fulfil the following properties:

1.

Every morphism
f:a\to b
factors as
f=me
with
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
.
This factorization is unique, up to a unique isomorphism, i.e. if there is some other factorization
f=m^{\prime}e^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
and
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, then there is a unique isomorphism
g
such that the following diagramm commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

2.

Both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
contain all isomorphisms and are closed under composition.

A factorization system is called proper, if all morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
are epi and all morphisms in
\mathcal{M}
are mono.

One example of such a factorization system in the category of finite undirected graphs is given by choosing
\mathcal{E}
as the (vertex) surjective graph homomorphisms, and
\mathcal{M}
as the strong graph embeddings.
The
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects of
H
in
G
are precisely the induced subgraphs of
G
that are isomorphic to
H
.
See §
C.3
for more details on this.

Except the properties directly visible in Definition
34
, we need one derived property (see
[
35
]
[Lemma 1.13] for a proof):

Fact 35

(Cancellation Properties)
.

Let (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
) be a factorization system and
f:a\to b
and
g:b\to c
be morphisms.
Then we have the following two implications:

•

If
gf\in\mathcal{E}
and
f\in\mathcal{E}
, then
g\in\mathcal{E}
.

•

If
gf\in\mathcal{M}
and
g\in\mathcal{M}
, then
f\in\mathcal{M}
.

Note that this implies that in a (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
) factorization system, for
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f
and
f^{\prime}
, whenever
f^{\prime}\prec f
, i.e. if there is some morphism
g
with
f^{\prime}=fg
, then
g
is already in
\mathcal{M}
.
In other words:
Being a
\mathcal{M}
-subobject is well-behaved under composition and cancellation.

Further, there are no non-trivial morphisms
f:a\to a
in
\mathcal{M}
:

Lemma 36
.

Let
C
be a locally small, finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered category and a proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system.
Then any morphism
f:a\to a
in
\mathcal{M}
is already an isomorphism.

Proof.

We consider the set of morphisms
\{f^{n}\,:\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}
is closed under composition, all these morphisms are in
\mathcal{M}
and thus are
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects of
a
.
By the pidgeonhole principle, combined with
C
being finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered, there must be some
n<m\in\mathbb{N}
with
f^{n}
and
f^{m}
being the same
\mathcal{M}
-subobject, i.e.
f^{n}=f^{m}g
with some isomorphism
g:a\to a
.
This leads to the following diagram with
f^{n}\mathrm{id}_{a}=f^{m}g
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{m-n}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{n}}

Due to
f^{n}\in\mathcal{M}
and the factorization system being proper, we conclude that
f^{n}
is a monomorphism and
f^{n-m}g=\mathrm{id}_{a}
, which is equivalent to
f^{n-m}=g^{-1}
.
It is now obvious that
f^{n-m-1}g=gf^{n-m-1}
is the inverse of
f
.
∎

Formally, it is not clear how to attach an object from some category
C
as an oracle to an NTM.
In order to do so, we denote by
{{C}_{=j}}
some set of objects from
C
that can be encoded as a
\mathrm{polylog}(j)
-bit oracle, i.e. as a subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathrm{bitsize}(j)}
for some efficiently computable function
\mathrm{bitsize}\in\mathrm{polylog}(j)
.
We require that for every object
c
in
C
, there is some
j\in\mathbb{N}
and a
c^{\prime}\in{{C}_{=j}}
, such that
c
and
c^{\prime}
are isomorphic.

Given a nondeterministic Turing machine
M
and an oracle object
c\in{{C}_{=j}}
,
we are interested in the number of accepting paths of
M
when given oracle access to the
j
-bit oracle encoding
c
.
We define the number of accepting paths of
M
on input
j\in\mathbb{N}
(given in binary
6
6

6

The precise nature of how
j
is given, what the bitsize of the encodings of elements from
{{C}_{=j}}
is and even whether the oracle is binary or not does not matter. The main importance is, that in polynomial time in the length of the input
M
can query the oracle at polynomially many positions out of exponentially many possible positions.
) with oracle access to
c
as
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{c}}(j)
.
Further we will also call
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{c}}(j)
a computation.

Our setup has two categories
C
and
P
, the ambient category and the category of
pure
objects respectively.
While we list here most of the general properties of both categories we assume in general, each lemma still explicitly lists the properties it uses to make them easier to follow.
Both categories are locally small and
P
is a full subcategory
7
7

7

Reminder, a subcategory is full if for all objects
a
and
b
that are in
P
, all morphisms between them in
C
are also part of
P
.
of
C
, closed under isomorphisms, i.e. if
a
and
b
are isomorphic objects in
C
, they are either both included in
P
or neither of them is.
Moreover there is a compatible proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system of
C
and
P
.
By compatible we mean some slight abuse of notation:
Not all morphisms in
\mathcal{M}
need to be part of
P
, however the restrictions of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
to the morphisms in
P
form themselves a proper factorization system of
P
.
To simplify notation we also call this restriction a proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system of
P
.
Furthermore
C
and
P
are both finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered and both have the joint
\mathcal{M}
-embedding property:

Definition 37
.

A locally small category
C
with a (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system has the joint
\mathcal{M}
-embedding property if for every two objects
a
and
b
, there is an object
c
such that there are
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f:a\to c
and
g:b\to c
.

Note that the resulting objects for the joint
\mathcal{M}
-embedding property of
P
also have to be contained in
P
.

The
\mathcal{M}
-well-poweredness now allows us to define motif parameters
\varphi
parallel to Definition
10
as linear combinations of
\mathcal{M}
-subobject counting functions
\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(c\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
for fixed objects
c
in
C
.
Note that whenever
c\cong c^{\prime}
, then clearly
\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(c\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)=\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(c^{\prime}\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
, so the different patterns in the linear combination are again always assumed to be pairwise non-isomorphic.
We call a pattern
P
-pure if they are in
P
and consequently we call
\varphi

P
-pure if all its patterns are
P
-pure.
As usual
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
denotes the counting function lifting
\varphi
to the corresponding promise type-2 function.

As usual, for some subclass
D
of objects from
C
that is closed under isomorphisms, we say that a motif parameter
\varphi
is
D
-good
if all its coefficients are nonnegative integers and all its patterns are from
D
.
If any such coefficient is negative it is instead called
D
-bad
.
If the domain
D
is clear from the context, we will simply call them
good
and
bad
respectively.

Before we get into the proof of our separation, we want to explain why we are restricting our motif parameters to be linear combinations.
If there are only finitely many morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
out of every object, then every monomial
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}t)
can be written as a linear combination with non-negative integer coefficients.
By extension, every polynomial over
\mathcal{M}
-subobject counting functions can be written as a linear combination.
We prove the statement for monomials by giving a characterization as sets, the wanted linearization then follows by taking the cardinality of all sets involved.

Lemma 38
.

Let
C
be a locally small category with finite coproducts and an (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system.
Then, assuming the axiom of choice, for any objects
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},t
from
C
we have
\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}t)\cong\bigsqcup_{b}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(b\mathop{\to}t)\times\{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{i})\in\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}b)\mid\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}\}
where
b
ranges over all non-isomorphic objects in
C
.

Proof.

For each isomorphism class pick one designated object
b
and for each
\mathcal{M}
-subobject of
b
in
t
, choose one designated morphism from
\mathcal{M}
, both using the axiom of choice.

We now directly give the bijection
\phi:\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}t)\to\bigsqcup_{b}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(b\mathop{\to}t)\times\{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{i})\in\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}b)\mid\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}\}
:

Given
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
with
f_{i}\in\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}t)
for each
i
, construct
f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}
.
Using the (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization, factorize
f
through some object
b
via
e:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}a_{k}\to b
from
\mathcal{E}
and
m:b\to t
from
\mathcal{M}
.
W.l.o.g.
8
8

8

Remember,
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
are both closed under composition and contain all isomorphisms. Further, the factorization is unique up to isomorphisms.
we can assume both
b
and
m
to be the corresponding designated object and morphism chosen by the axiom of choice.
Since the factorization is unique up to isomorphism, this makes
(b,m)
unique.
This leads to the following commutative diagram for each
i
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}}

Since
f_{i}
and
m
are both in
\mathcal{M}
, this implies that
h_{i}:=e\iota_{i}
is in
\mathcal{M}
by the cancellation property of a factorization system.
Due to
e=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
, we see that
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
is in
\mathcal{E}
.
Our bijection thus maps
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
to
(m,(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}))
.
Note that this is well-defined: if there are morphisms
f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{k}
from
\mathcal{M}
and isomorphism
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
with
f^{\prime}_{i}=f_{i}g_{i}
, then we get
h^{\prime}_{i}=h_{i}g_{i}
and thus output the same
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects.

To define
\Phi^{-1}
, let
b
be some designated object in
C
, let
m
be the designated morphism of some
\mathcal{M}
-subobject of
b
in
t
and let
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{i}\mathop{\to}b)
with
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}
is closed under composition, we see that each of the
mh_{i}
is in
\mathcal{M}
.
The bijection then outputs
(mh_{1},\ldots,mh_{k})
.
This function is well-defined by the same argument as above.

Bijectivity now follows from the fact that
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}=m\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
.
∎

Even more, we can even prove that the coefficients in the linear combination are non-negative integers if the polynomial, given in the binomial basis, has non-negative integer coefficients (potentially leading to negative coefficients in front of the polynomial in monomial basis).
For example
\varphi(t)=\binom{\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}t)}{2}=\frac{(\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}t))^{2}}{2}-\frac{\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}t)}{2}
can be written as a linear combination of
\mathcal{M}
-subobject counting functions with non-negative integer coefficients.

Corollary 39
.

Let
C
be a locally small category with finite coproducts and a proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system.
Then, assuming the axiom of choice, for any objects
a
and
t
from
C
we have
\binom{\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}t)}{k}\cong\bigsqcup_{b}\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(b\mathop{\to}t)\times\{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{i})\in\binom{\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}b)}{k}\mid\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}\}
where
b
ranges over all non-isomorphic objects in
C
.

Proof.

In the proof of Lemma
38
in the construction of the bijection, whenever
\mathcal{M}
only contains monos, from
f_{i}=f_{j}
, we automatically obtain
h_{i}=h_{j}
and vice-versa.
Restricting to only the cases where the morphisms are pairwise disjoint gives the wanted bijection.
∎

We now continue on to the proof of our separation by first generalizing the notion of set-instantiators:

Definition 40

(Set-instantiator against
(M,c,\varphi)
)
.

Let
C
and
P
be locally small, finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered categories with a compatible (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system and where
P
is a full subcategory of
C
that is closed under isomorphism.

Let
M
be a nondeterministic Turing machine,
let
c\in C
and
let
\varphi
be a
P
-pure motif parameter.

Let
\top
be a symbolic top element above the
\mathcal{M}
-subobject poset
\mathcal{P}(c)
, i.e.,
\top\not\prec f
for all
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f\in\mathcal{P}(c)
.
A
set-instantiator

\mathcal{S}\!I
is a triple of

\circ
some
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\circ
an instantiation function
\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}:\mathcal{P}(c)\to{{C}_{=j}}
, and

\circ
a perception function
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\mathcal{P}(c)\cup\{\top\}
,

such that the following property holds for all
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f\in\mathcal{P}(c)
.

\bullet

\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}
is an accepting path for the computation
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(f)}}(j)

iff
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\prec f
, and

\bullet

\varphi(\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(f)))=\varphi(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}f)
.

Again, we think of perceptions of an accepting computation path of
M
to be the
\mathcal{M}
-subobject of
c
that is observed by
M
through the oracle queries, while computation paths that do not accept are given perception
\top
.
Unfortunately there is no generalized construction for set-instantiators as this has to heavily depend on the actual encoding of the objects as oracle layers.
For example consider an encoding where every object is encoded as an oracle layer containing exactly one
1
.
Then
M
can determine all information about the object by guessing the position of that single
1
and then querying the oracle once.
However, this encoding is not particularly natural and set-instantiators often do exist when encoded naturally.
See sections §
C.4
and §
C.5
for two more set-instantiators.

Again, we have the problem that for
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects
f
and
g
with
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}f\cong\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g
we could have
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(f)}}(j)\neq\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(g)}}(j)
, which we do not want to happen in a good function.
We thus want to construct an instantiation function that no longer has this problem.
We achieve this by creating a very large set-instantiator and then restricting ourselves to some small part of the set-instantiator where we can enforce this property.

Ensuring this structure is achieved by requiring
C
to have the Ramsey property, relative to
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects.

Definition 41
.

A locally small category
C
with a (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system is called
\mathcal{M}
-Ramsey, if for every
a,b\in C
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
, there is some
c\in C
, such that for every
\Phi:\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c)\to\{0,\ldots,t\}
, there is a
f_{\Phi}\in\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(b\mathop{\to}c)
with the property that
\Phi
is constant when restricted to inputs from
\{g\in\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c)\,:\,g\prec f_{\Phi}\}
.

See
[
12
]
for some categorical conditions that imply the Ramsey property.

We extend this to colorings of all
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects of
b
, not just those with their domain being
a
:

Proposition 42

(Ramsey Theorem)
.

Let
C
be an
\mathcal{M}
-Ramsey, locally small and finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered category with a (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system.

Let
b\in C
and let
t\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.
Then there is a
c\in C
, such that for every
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(c)\to\{0,\ldots,t\}
there is a
f_{\Phi}\in\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(b\mathop{\to}c)
with the property, if
g,h\prec f_{\Phi}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g\cong\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}h
, then
\Phi(g)=\Phi(h)
.

Proof.

The
\mathcal{P}(b)
is finite due to
C
being finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered.
Now inductively apply the property that
C
is
\mathcal{M}
-Ramsey on
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
.
∎

This theorem can now be used to ensure that for every polynomial-time NTM there must be some small
9
9

9

Small in the colloquial sense of small, meaning some “small” finite set, not in the sense of category theory where small simply means a set.
set of inputs where the NTM behaves like a good function.
The instantiation function for the inputs where the NTM behaves like a good function now doesn’t instantiate individual subobjects anymore, but instead only instantiates objects (the domains of the subobjects).
This change is natural since the computed function is now locally a motif parameter and thus invariant under isomorphisms, i.e. the precise subobject doesn’t matter, only its domain).

Lemma 43
.

Let
C
and
P
be locally small and finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered categories with a compatible (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system, where
P
is a full subcategory of
C
that is closed under isomorphisms and where
C
is
\mathcal{M}
-Ramsey.

Let
\varphi
be a
P
-pure motif parameter, let
M
be any nondeterministic polynomial-time Turing machine computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
and let
b\in C
.
If for every
c\in C
there are set-instantiators against
(M,c,\varphi)
,
then there is some
j\in\mathbb{N}
, a function
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\to{{C}_{=j}}
and a
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
-good function
\Psi
with
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{a)}}(j)=\Psi(a)
and
\varphi(a))=\varphi(a)
for every
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
.

Proof.

We invoke Proposition
42
for
b
and
t:=\max\{\varphi(a)\,:\,a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\}
to obtain
c\in C
.
We now want to color
\mathcal{P}(c)
according to the behaviour of
M
on the elements of it, in particular how many accepting paths query each specific
\mathcal{M}
-subobject of
c
.
Before we can do this we have to first embed all potential
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects in
\mathcal{P}(c)
as oracles.
In order to do this we construct a set-instantiator
\mathcal{S}\!I
against
(M,c,\varphi)
to obtain a
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{S}\!I
and
\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}
as in Definition
40
.

For
g\in\mathcal{P}(c)
define
\Phi(g):=\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}(g)}}(j)
.
Note that

\Phi(g)\ =\ \bigl|\bigl\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}\,:\,\textup{{{perc}}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)\prec g\bigr\}\bigr|=\sum_{h\prec g}\,\bigl|\big\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}\,:\,\textup{{perc}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)=h\big\}\bigr|.

(7)

Using
\Phi
as a coloring for Proposition
42
, we obtain
f_{\Phi}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}f_{\Phi}=b
.
Note that
\Phi(g)
now only depends on the isomorphism type of the domain of
g
for all
g\prec f_{\Phi}
.
By induction we see that the number

\big|\big\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{*}\,:\,\textup{{perc}}_{\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(\tau)=g\big\}\big|

also depends only on the isomorphism type of
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g
, whenever
g\prec f_{\Phi}
.
Denote this number by
\alpha_{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g}
and set
\Psi(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g):=\Phi(g)
.
The function
\Phi
is well-defined and invariant under isomorphisms of
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g
only for
g\prec f_{\Phi}
, thus we consider the domain of
\Phi
to be
\{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g\,:\,g\prec f_{\Phi}\}=\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
.
This means that (
7
) simplifies:

\Psi(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g)\ =\ \sum_{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}h}\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}h\mathop{\to}\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g)\alpha_{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g}

(8)

where the sum is over all non-isomorphic domains
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}h
for
h\in\mathcal{P}(b)
.
This implies that
\Phi
is
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
-good.

Remains to define the function
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\to{{C}_{=j}}
.
Let
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
, then
a):={\textup{{$\mathcal{S}\!I$}}}(g_{\Phi})
for some
g_{\Phi}\prec f_{\Phi}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}g_{\Phi}\cong a
.
The property
\varphi(a))=\varphi(a)
now directly follows from
\mathcal{S}\!I
being a set-instantiator.
∎

Note that Lemma
43
only guarantees the local behaviour of
M
to be
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)
-good.
This leaves two possibilities for contradictions: Either the local behaviour is even
(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\cap P)
-good, or the function grows too quickly for non
P
-pure inputs.
This next Witness Theorem shows that in the first case we can always find an object that proves that
M
does not compute the correct function using a simple linear algebra argument.

Theorem 44

(The Witness Theorem)
.

Let
P
be a locally small, finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered category with a proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system and the joint
\mathcal{M}
-embedding property.

Fix a bad
\varphi:P\to\mathbb{N}
.
Then there exists
b\in P
such that for
every
(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\cap P)
-good function
\Psi:(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\cap P)\to\mathbb{N}
,
there exists
w\in(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\cap P)

with
\Psi(w)\neq\varphi(w)
.

Proof.

Use the joint embedding property of
P
on
\textup{supp}(\varphi)
to obtain an object
b\in P
, such that there are morphisms
a\to b
in
\mathcal{M}
for all
a\in\textup{supp}(\varphi)
.
We now consider
P^{\prime}:=\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(b)\cap P
, keeping only one representative per isomorphism class.
We now prove that the functions
\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
for
a\in P^{\prime}
are linearly independent:
Consider the evaluation matrix
A
with rows and columns indexed by objects from
P^{\prime}
, sorted by a total extension of the poset
\mathcal{P}(b)
and values
A_{ij}:=\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(i\mathop{\to}j)
.
This is well-defined by Lemma
36
: if there are morphisms from
\mathcal{M}
,
f:a\to a^{\prime}
and
g:a^{\prime}\to a
, then
a
and
a^{\prime}
are isomorphic and thus only one of them is part of
P^{\prime}
.
If this matrix is invertible this directly implies that the
\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
(i.e. the rows) are linearly independent.

We now prove that
A
is an upper-diagonal matrix with a
1
s on the diagonal and thus invertible.
The matrix
A
is upper-diagonal since we’ve ordered the rows and columns accordingly, combined with Lemma
36
.
Remaining are the
1
s on the diagonal:
For any object
a
, the identity morphisms
\mathrm{id}_{a}
is contained in
\mathcal{M}
and thus there is at least one
\mathcal{M}
-subobject in
\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}a)
.
Furthermore every morphism
f:a\to a
in
\mathcal{M}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
36
and thus the same
\mathcal{M}
-subobject as
\mathrm{id}_{a}
.

We finish the proof of the Witness Theorem by noticing that the linear independence implies that the patterns and coefficients of any function
\Psi
with support
\textup{supp}(\Psi)\subseteq P^{\prime}
are uniquely determined by the evaluations
\Psi
on
P^{\prime}
.
Thus there exists a witness
a\in P^{\prime}
with
\Psi(a)\neq\varphi(a)
, since
\Psi
is good while
\varphi
is bad (thus, in particular,
\Psi
and
\varphi
are not the same function).
∎

We need one last condition on
C
and
P
:
A procedure that, given some
c
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
allows to construct a “bigger” object
c^{\prime}
that cannot be distinguished by any
P
-pure motif parameters, but every non
P
-pure basis function with patterns from
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c)
evaluates to a value bigger than
k
.
Formally:

Definition 45
.

Let
C
and
P
be locally small and finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered categories with a compatible (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system, where
P
is a full subcategory of
C
that is closed under isomorphisms.

We say that
C
and
P
fulfill the
blowup property
, if for every object
c
in
C
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
there is some
c^{\prime}
in
C
, such that

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c^{\prime})

\displaystyle>k

for every
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c)\setminus P

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c^{\prime})

for every
a\in P

In the case of graphs, this was achieved by adding isolated vertices.

With this last tool we are finally able to lead the existence of polynomial-time NTMs computing bad ordered graph motif parameters to a contradiction.

Lemma 46
.

Let
C
and
P
be two categories with the following properties:

•

P
is a full subcategory of
C
, closed under isomorphism.

•

C
and
P
have a compatible proper (
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{M}
)-factorization system.

•

C
and
P
are both locally small.

•

C
and
P
are both finitely
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered.

•

C
and
P
both have the joint
\mathcal{M}
-embedding property.

•

C
is
\mathcal{M}
-Ramsey.

•

C
and
P
fulfill the blowup property.

Let
M
be any nondeterministic polynomial-time Turing machine computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
for some bad
P
-pure motif parameter
\varphi
.
If for every
c\in C
there are set-instantiators against
(M,c,\varphi)
, then there is a
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
c\in{{C}_{=j}}
such that
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{c}}(j)\neq\varphi(c)
.

Proof.

We invoke the Witness Theorem
44
with our
\varphi

to obtain a
c_{1}\in P
as in the theorem.
Using the blowup property, construct
c_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c_{2})

\displaystyle>\max\big\{\varphi(b)\,:\,b\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\cap P\big\}

for every
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\setminus P

(9)

\displaystyle\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c_{2})

\displaystyle=\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c_{1})

\displaystyle\text{for every $a\in P$}\,.

(10)

We now use Lemma
43
to obtain a
j\in\mathbb{N}
, a function
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{2})\to{{C}_{=j}}
and a
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{2})
-good function
\Psi
with
\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{a)}}(j)=\Psi(a)
and
\varphi(a))=\varphi(a)
for every
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{2})
.

There are now two possibilities to find the required counterexample
w
:
If there are no basis functions present in
\Psi
with a pattern in
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\setminus P
, we can invoke the Witness Theorem again, however if any of these basis functions are present we can construct a direct counterexample.

We first handle the case where such a basis function is present, so let the coefficient of
\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}\,\star\,)
in
\Psi
be positive for some
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\setminus P
.
Then

\Psi(c_{2})\geq\#\mathrm{Sub}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\mathop{\to}c_{2})>\max\big\{\varphi(b)\,:\,b\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\cap P\big\}\geq\varphi(c_{1})=\varphi(c_{2})

by (
9
) and (
10
), combined with the fact that
\varphi
is
P
-pure and thus only has patterns from
P
.
Our counterexample in this case is
w:=c_{2}
.

Otherwise assume that no such basis function is present.
Then
\widetilde{\Psi}
obtained from
\Psi
by restricting to
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})
(i.e. setting all coefficients of
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{2})\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})
to zero), is
(\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\cap P)
-good, which is a requirement for the second part of the Witness Theorem
44
(we already used it to obtain
c_{1}
).
Furthermore for the restriction to
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})
we have
\widetilde{\Psi}_{|\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})}=\Psi_{|\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})}
.
We invoke the second part of the Witness Theorem
44
to find a point

w\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{P}(c_{1})\cap P
with
\Psi(w)=\widetilde{\Psi}(w)\neq\varphi(w)
which is our counterexample.

Independent of how we have obtained our
w
we observe

\#\textup{{{acc}}}_{M^{w)}}(j)=\Psi(w)\neq\varphi(w),

(11)

which completes the proof.
∎

Diagonalizing over all polynomial-time NTMs computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
now leads to our general hardness result:

Theorem 47
.

Let
C
and
P
be as in Lemma
46
and let
\varphi
be a
P
-pure motif parameter.
If set-instatiators against
(M,c,\varphi)
exist for all nondeterministic polynomial-time Turing machines
M
computing
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}
and objects
c\in C
, then
\operatorname{Eval}_{\varphi}\notin\mathsf{Pr}\text{-}{\textup{{{\#P}}}}^{\hbox to4.07pt{\vbox to4.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.03436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,1.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.29 0 C 2.29 1.27 1.27 2.29 0 2.29 C -1.27 2.29 -2.29 1.27 -2.29 0 C -2.29 -1.27 -1.27 -2.29 0 -2.29 C 1.27 -2.29 2.29 -1.27 2.29 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
unless
\varphi
is good.

Proof.

There are two possibilities for
\varphi
to not be good:
Either
\varphi
is bad or
\varphi
has a non-integer coefficient.

We first treat the case where
\varphi
is bad.
Assume for the sake of contradiction
